Projective Transformations for Regularized Central Force Dynamics: Hamiltonian Formulation
Joseph T.A. Peterson, Manoranjan Majji, John L. Junkins

Abstract
This work introduces a Hamiltonian approach to regularization and linearization of central force particle dynamics through a new canonical extension of the so-called “projective decomposition”. The regularization scheme is formulated within the framework of classic analytical Hamiltonian dynamics as a redundant-dimensional canonical/symplectic coordinate transformation, combined with an evolution parameter transformation, on extended phase space. By considering a generalized version of the standard projective decomposition, we obtain a family of such canonical transformations which differ at the momentum level. From this family of transformations, a preferred canonical coordinate set is chosen that possesses a simple, elegant, connection to the particle’s local reference frame. Using this transformation, closed-form solutions are readily obtained for inverse square and inverse cubic radial forces (or any superposition thereof) on any finite-dimensional Euclidean space. From these solutions, a new set of orbit elements for Kepler-Coulomb dynamics is derived, along with their variational equations for arbitrary perturbations (singularity-free in all cases besides rectilinear motion). Governing equations are numerically validated for the classic two-body problem incorporating the J 2 J_{2} gravitational perturbation.



1.  INTRODUCTION

In the Newtonian gravity model for gravitational constant
G
, the non-linear equation of motion for two bodies of mass
m_{a}
and
m_{b}
in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
is given (after reduction to 3-
dof
) by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hskip-1.70717pt{{\mkern 1.5mu\accentset{\mbox{\scriptsize{.}}}{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\accentset{\mbox{\scriptsize{.}}}{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.41112pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}}{r^{3}}{\vec{{r}}}\;+\;{\textbf{{{a}}}}\quad,

\displaystyle{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{3.44444pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}=G(m_{a}+m_{b})

where
{\vec{{r}}}_{t}={r}_{b}(t)-{r}_{a}(t)
is the displacement vector from
m_{a}
to
m_{b}
with norm
r:=|\!|{\vec{{r}}}|\!|
, where
{\mkern 1.5mu\accentset{\mbox{\scriptsize{.}}}{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\vec{{r}}}
is the velocity, where
\nabla_{\hskip-1.70717pt{{\mkern 1.5mu\accentset{\mbox{\scriptsize{.}}}{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\accentset{\mbox{\scriptsize{.}}}{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}
is the acceleration (classically written
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{}}{\mathrm{d}{t}^{2}}{\vec{{r}}}
or
{\mkern 1.5mu\accentset{\mbox{\scriptsize..}}{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}
),
and where
a
is the perturbing force vector per unit reduced mass,
m:=\tfrac{m_{a}m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}
, arising from any non-Keplerian forces (e.g., gravitational perturbations, thrust, drag, radiation pressure, etc.).
1

1

Note in eq.(

) that we have already reduced the initial
6
-
dof
problem of two particles to an equivalent 3-
dof
problem, as usual. It is often further assumed that
m_{b}<<m_{a}
such that
m_{b}
orbits around
m_{a}
, which is then the approximately stationary “central body”. In that case,
{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{3.44444pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}\approx Gm_{a}
is approximately the gravitational parameter of the central body (often denoted
\mu
) and
m\approx m_{b}
is approximately the mass of the orbiting body.

The above vector equation may be expressed using a wide variety of coordinates and considerable work in celestial mechanics has focused on
developing coordinate transformations that lead to simplifying or linearizing effects in the equations of motion or removing the singularity at
r=0

[
1
,
2
,
3

4

]
. This process is broadly referred to as
regularization
and often incorporates both a coordinate transformation
and a transformation of the evolution parameter to something other than time
(see eq.(
3
)).
Generally speaking, such methods can be grouped into two categories: those which employ Hamiltonian formalism with canonical coordinates — that is, symplectic coordinates, also called Darboux coordinates — and those which do not (of those using Hamiltonian formalism, we might further delineate by whether the transformation is formulated and analyzed in an analytical mechanics framework or a symplectic geometry framework, but, we will set that topic aside for the moment).
A primary advantage of the Hamiltonian formulation is that the process of identifying conserved quantities and obtaining linearizing coordinate transformations is formalized within one process through use of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation
[
13
,
14
]
. It also allows for perturbed problems to be treated using various canonical perturbation methods such as Von Ziepel’s method (as done by Brouwer) or Lie Series methods (as done by Deprit, Hori, and Kamel)

[
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
.
Furthermore, since the phase flow of a system expressed in canonical coordinates is, by definition, a symplectomorphism on coordinate space, such coordinates are the natural choice for symplectic integration methods.

Projective Decomposition: The General Idea.

A seminal example of regularization of eq.(
1
) which typically does
not
employ Hamiltonian or symplectic methods is the so-called
projective decomposition
used by Burdet, Sperling, Vitins and others
[
3
,
4
,
19
,
8
]
.
This method uses a redundant set of four configuration coordinates, along with a transformation of the evolution parameter, to transform the
three
-dimensional perturbed Kepler problem in eq.(
1
) into a
four
-dimensional perturbed harmonic oscillator which, for the unperturbed problem (
{\textbf{{{a}}}}=0
), is fully linear. Although slightly different formulations exist — and Schumacher has given a detailed overview of several of them
[
1
]
—
the general process is summarized as follows:

1.

Let
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
denote the cartesian coordinate representations of the displacement vector
{\vec{{r}}}\in\mathbb{E}^{3}
in some
orthonormal inertial
basis, with
{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
the corresponding cartesian representation of
a
, such that the two-body equations of motion, eq.(
1
), are written simply as

\displaystyle\ddot{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.16998pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}}{r^{3}}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,+\,{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

2.

Specify a point transformation
\psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\ni({\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},z)\mapsto{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
for
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=\psiup({\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},z)
, such that
{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}/r={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
are the three inertial cartesian components of the radial unit vector and
z
is a single coordinate related to the radial distance,
r
, by
r=z^{n}
for some constant
n\neq 0

(often,
n=\pm 1
).
2
2

2

Typically, one uses either
n=1
such that
z=r
, or
n=-1
such that
z=\tfrac{1}{r}
.

This is often called the
projective decomposition
:

\displaystyle{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,\psiup({\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},z)\,=\,z^{n}{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\;\;,\;\;\hskip 18.49988pt{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\;\;,\;\;z=r^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}}

(7)

3.

Transform the evolution parameter from time,
t
, to a parameter,
s
, related to
t
by some specified differential relation
(examples
3
3

3

Some common evolution parameters used for regularization of the Kepler problem are given below, where
{\ell}
is angular momentum magnitude (the below
\tau
,

, and

correspond to the true, eccentric, and mean, anomaly

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\text{d}t=r\text{d}s&\!\!{\text{\footnotesize{(unnamed)}}}\\[3.0pt]
\text{d}t=r^{2}\text{d}s&\!\!{\text{\footnotesize{(unnamed)}}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\text{d}t=\tfrac{r^{2}}{{\ell}}\text{d}\tau\;\;{\text{\footnotesize{(true anomaly)}}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\text{d}t=r\sqrt{a/{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{3.44444pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}}\,\text{d}{\textit{{{e}}}}=\tfrac{r}{\sqrt{-2E_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}}\text{d}{\textit{{{e}}}}&\!\!{\text{\footnotesize{(eccentric anomaly)}}}\\[3.0pt]

):

\displaystyle\acute{t}\,=\,\tfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}s}\,=\,f({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},\dot{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}},t)

(14)

4.

Substitute eq.(
7
) into eq.(
1
) and then convert all
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
terms to
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}
terms using
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\tfrac{1}{\acute{t}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}
for the specified
\acute{t}
. The end result — for appropriate choices of
n
and
\acute{t}
— can be used to obtain four equations for
{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
and
z
which have the general form

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}&\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\!\acute{\vphantom{{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}$\hss}{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}+\omega^{2}{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,{\vphantom{c}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{c}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}+\text{ (perturbations)}\\[4.0pt]
&\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{z}}\!\acute{\vphantom{z}}$\hss}z+\omega^{2}z\,=\,b+\text{ (perturbations)}\end{array}

where
\acute{\square}=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}s}
and where
\omega
,
b
, and
{\vphantom{c}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{c}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
are all either numeric constants or integrals of motion for the unperturbed Kepler problem. Thus, if no perturbations are present, then the above are the equations of a linear harmonic oscillator with constant frequency
\omega
and constant driving “forces”
{\vphantom{c}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{c}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
and
b
. The particular meaning/nature of these parameters depends on the choice of
n
in eq.(
7
) and the choice of
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}s}=f({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},\dot{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}},t)
in eq.(
14
). For example, Burdet used
{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
and
z=\tfrac{1}{r}
(that is,
n=-1
) and regularized the time using
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}s}=r^{2}
, leading to
b={\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{3.44444pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}
,
{\vphantom{c}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{c}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{0}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{0}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
, and
\omega^{2}={\ell}^{2}
— where
{\ell}
is the angular momentum magnitude per unit reduced mass
[
3
,
4
]
. Vitins used the same coordinates but with
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}s}=\tfrac{r^{2}}{{\ell}}
— making
s
the true anomaly up to some additive constant — which leads to
\omega^{2}=1
,
b=\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.41112pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}}{{\ell}^{2}}
, and
{\vphantom{c}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{c}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{0}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{0}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

[
19
]
. Vitins then made a subsequent point transformation from projective coordinates to quaternionic attitude coordinates (Euler parameters) for the local vertical, local horizontal (LVLH) frame. He presented a closed-form Kepler solution in these quaternionic coordinates and used the initial conditions to develop a set of eight orbit elements.

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

Terminology.

Although the term "projective" appears frequently in this work, it should seldom be interpreted too formally.
In the mathematical context of projective spaces or projective geometry, terms like "projective transformation" and "projective coordinates" have definitions which do not always align with our, more relaxed, use of such terms.
This clash of terminology arises because, in the celestial mechanics literature, point transformations of the above form in eq.(
7
) are commonly,
and appropriately
4
4

4

Such names are indeed appropriate: from eq.(
7
), it may be evident so some that there is certainly a connection to be made between the various projective decompositions/transformations of celestial mechanics and the mathematical framework of projective geometry (eq.(
7
) indeed implies a connection to projective spaces).
,
referred to as a “projective decomposition” or a “projective transformation” (we use either interchangeably) and the coordinates — arbitrarily denoted
({\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},z)
above — are then referred to as as “projective coordinates” (Deprit et al. attribute this to Ferrándiz
[
20
]
).

Regularizing Canonical Transformations.

The regularizing projective transformations described above do not use Hamiltonian methods. Part of this is due to the fact that all of them employ
redundant
coordinates — artificially increasing the dimension of the configuration space — which is not easily compatible with classic Hamiltonian formalism. However, prior work has explored methods of overcoming this obstacle.
In particular, Ferrándiz considered the general problem of canonical transformations increasing the number of coordinates and used this to extend (or "lift") the classic point transformation for Burdet-type projective coordinates to a canonical transformation (i.e., symplectic coordinate transformation), which adds two redundant dimensions to the resulting "projective coordinate phase space"
[
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
.
We will refer to this as the
Burdet-Ferrándiz (BF) transformation
, following the naming convention of
[
20
]
where this same transformation was revisited.
Within the realm of celestial mechanics, the
Kustaanheimo-Stiefel (KS) transformation
is perhaps the most well-known example of regularization employing Hamiltonian methods in conjunction with redundant coordinates
[
5
,
6
,
25
,
20
,
26
]
. Although the physical or geometric interpretation of the KS variables is often hidden behind the matrix algebra in which they are typically presented, several sources have discussed their close connection to quaternions
[
20
,
27
]
.

Outline & Contributions.

This work develops a new regularization scheme for central force particle dynamics in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
which makes use of the extended phase space formulation of Hamiltonian mechanics. The regularization requires adding a redundant dimension to configuration space (and thus two additional dimensions to phase space).
The transformation — which includes a transformation of the evolution parameter — fully linearizes Euclidean particle dynamics for any inverse square and/or inverse cubic radial forces.
The Keplerian phase space solution is then used to develop a new set of canonical orbital elements, along with their governing equations of motion in the presence of arbitrary perturbations. The
J_{2}
-perturbed 2BP is used for numerical verification. In more detail:

•

First, before discussing anything to do with regularization or orbital motion, we address the general problem of transforming a given Hamiltonian system from a minimal coordinate representation to some new, non-minimal/redundant, coordinate representation. Though the result of our method agrees in the end with with Ferrándiz’s treatment of the same problem, we take a different approach that we feel is more transparent and easily understood by anyone acquainted with analytical dynamics and Hamilton’s principle. In addition, we expand upon Ferrándiz’s treatment by allowing for time-dependent transformations.

•

Next, this method is used to develop a generalized formulation of the BF transformation (first introduced by the authors in
[
28
]
). We use a coordinate point transformation,
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\ni({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)\mapsto{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, given by
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)=u^{n}|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|^{m}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
— for arbitrary scalar constants
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
0\neq n\in{\mathbb{R}}
— whose cotangent lift (that is, canonical extension) induces a corresponding family of canonical transformations,
{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\ni({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{u})\mapsto({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
. By choosing
m=0
and
n=\pm 1
, one recovers the BF transformation
[
22
]
.
We instead choose to focus on the case that
m=-1
and
n=-1
(that is,
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}/(|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|u)
) which leads to several differences compared to the BF transformation (notably, different conjugate momenta coordinates). Furthermore, before addressing specifically the Kepler problem, we expand on previous work by considering arbitrary central forces and perturbations, including non-conservative forces. It is shown that our transformation fully linearizes Euclidean particle dynamics for any radial force field of the form
\pm\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.16998pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}_{1}}{r^{2}}{\mkern 1.5mu\hat{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}\pm\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.16998pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}_{2}}{r^{3}}{\mkern 1.5mu\hat{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}
for any
{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{3.09999pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}_{1},{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{3.09999pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
(this clearly includes the Kepler force,
-\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.16998pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}}{r^{2}}{\mkern 1.5mu\hat{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}
).

•

Next, we turn our attention to the Kepler problem (easily realized as a special case of the preceding developments) and give closed-form solutions and state transition matrices in terms of evolution parameters
s
and
\tau
(where
{\mathrm{d}}t=r^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s
and
{\mathrm{d}}t=(r^{2}/{\ell}){\mathrm{d}}\tau
).
The
J_{2}
-perturbed 2BP is formulated using the canonical projective coordinates and used to numerically verify the Equations of motion.

•

Lastly, the Kepler solutions in canonical projective coordinates are then used to introduce a new set of eight corresponding orbit elements for the 2BP, along with their governing variational equations for arbitrarily-perturbed motion. These elements and equations of motion singularity-free in all cases besides rectilinear motion. The
J_{2}
-perturbed 2BP is again used for numerical verification.

1.1  Summary of This Work

We now present, without derivation, a brief summary of the projective coordinate canonical transformation and its application to regularization of central force particle dynamics and, in particular, the Kepler Problem.
This work is written in a coordinate-dominant manner meant to be familiar for readers acquainted with classic analytical dynamics (cf.
[
13
,
14
,
29
,
30
]
). Matrix notation is used frequently, as is common in astrodynamics and engineering. Index notation and summation are also occasionally used. Although many aspects of this work are elegantly expressed in the language of differential geometry, such developments are omitted from this work for the sake of brevity.

1.1.1  Canonical Transformations from Minimal to Redundant Coordinates.

We first treat the general problem of constructing a canonical transformation that is induced by a point transformation from minimal to redundant coordinates.
We consider some phase space with canonical coordinates
({\vphantom{x}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{x}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{\piup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\piup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and known Hamiltonian
{{K}}({\vphantom{x}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{x}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{\piup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\piup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)
and show that, given some specified point transformation
{\vphantom{x}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{x}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{x}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{x}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)
— where
{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
are some new set of
redundant
coordinates — along with
m
specified “constraint” relations,
{\vphantom{\varphiup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\varphiup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then we can construct a new set of redundant phase space coordinates
({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2(n+m)}
and Hamiltonian
{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)
. The momenta transformation is given by

\displaystyle{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=B^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}\begin{pmatrix}{\vphantom{\piup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\piup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\
{\vphantom{\lambdaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\lambdaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\leftrightarrow\ \begin{pmatrix}{\vphantom{\piup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\piup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\
{\vphantom{\lambdaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\lambdaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\end{pmatrix}=B^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{--T}}}}}{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}

,

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{x}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{x}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{\varphiup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\varphiup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+m)\times(n+m)}

(17)

for Lagrange multipliers
{\vphantom{\lambdaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\lambdaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The new Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\;=\;{{K}}\,-\,{\vphantom{\lambdaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\lambdaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{\varphiup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\varphiup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial t}\,-\,{\vphantom{\piup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\piup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{x}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{x}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial t}

(18)

where the right-hand-side of the above must be expressed in terms of
{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
and
{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
.

1.1.2  The (Generalized) Projective Decomposition Extended to a Canonical Transformation

We now use
{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
to denote our projective coordinates, and
{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=({\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
to denote their conjugate momentum coordinates.

As outlined in eq.(
1
) through eq.(
4
), the classic projective decomposition is given by a point transformation
({\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},z)\mapsto{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=z^{n}{\vphantom{y}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{y}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
, (usually with
n=\pm 1
) where
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
are inertial cartesian coordinates.
We begin in section
2.2
with a generalized version of the projective decomposition, given by
({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)\mapsto{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=u^{n}|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|^{m}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
, and then “lift”, or, “extend” this point transformation to a canonical transformation,
({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\mapsto({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})
. We develop the Hamiltonian formulation for particle dynamics in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
— including arbitrary conservative and non-conservative forces — using the redundant canonical projective coordinates,
({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{8}
.

Original Cartesian Coordinate Formulation.

Since we use the Hamiltonian formulation,
rather than starting with the three second-order equations of motion for configuration space coordinates — expressed in cartesian coordinates simply as
\ddot{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial V}{\uppartial r}+{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}
— we instead start with
the Hamiltonian (per unit reduced mass) and the corresponding six first-order equations of motion for canonical phase space coordinates. Letting
({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
be cartesian position and velocity coordinates in any orthonormal inertial basis, this is given simply by:

\displaystyle{{K}}({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\nu}^{2}\,+\,V({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial{{K}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[5.0pt]
\dot{{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial{{K}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,+\,{\vphantom{{\text{\textGamma}}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\text{\textGamma}}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V}{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,+\,{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}\end{array}

where
{\nu}:=|\!|{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|
,
where
V({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)
is the cartesian coordinate representation of some (possibly time-dependent) potential field, and where
{\vphantom{{\text{\textGamma}}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\text{\textGamma}}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is the generalized
non-conservative
forces which, in the case of inertial cartesian phase space coordinates, is nothing more than the cartesian coordinate vector of any non-conservative forces (e.g., thrust, drag),
{\vphantom{{\text{\textGamma}}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\text{\textGamma}}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
We will consider the case that potential is comprised of a term,
V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(r)
, dependant only on the radial distance,
r=|\!|{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|
, and a second term,
V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)
accounting for all other conservative perturbations. The above dynamics are then equivalent to

\displaystyle V({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)\,=\,V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(r)\,+\,V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},t)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\dot{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}={\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\quad,\quad\dot{{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial r}{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\,+\,{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\quad,\qquad{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,:=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}

(21)

where
r:=|\!|{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|
,
{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}:={\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}/r
, and where we have defined
{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
to be the inertial cartesian coordinate vector of the
total
perturbing force (per unit reduced mass). For the general two body problem, we simply set
V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=-\tfrac{{\mkern-4.0mu\textnormal{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raisebox{2.41112pt}{\scriptsize{--}}\cr$\mkern 4.0mu{\mathrm{k}}$\cr}}}\vphantom{k}}}{r}
and, for the Kepler problem, we further set
{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0
.

Canonical Transformation for Projective Coordinates.

For the Hamiltonian formulation of the projective decomposition, we then transform from the above
({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
to
new, redundant, canonical coordinates,
({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})=({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{8}
, where
{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
will, eventually, be the classic projective coordinates.
Rather than use the classic point transformation for the projective coordinates seen in eq.(
7
), we instead use the following generalization (where
q=|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|\neq|\!|{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}|\!|
):

\displaystyle\psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\;,\hskip 18.49988pt{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,\psiup({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)\,=\,u^{n}q^{m}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

\displaystyle{\text{\footnotesize{subject to}}}\;\;\varphi({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})=\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}-1=0

(22)

where we may choose
n,m\in\mathbb{R}
to be any constants we like so long as
n\neq 0
.
Note that we want the coordinates
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
to be the inertial cartesian coordinates of the radial unit vector,
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
, and thus has unit magnitude. As such, the factor of
q^{m}
in the above would seem utterly pointless. It will turn out to be quite important.
The four momenta conjugate to
({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)
, denoted
({\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
, are then obtained as in eq.(
17
), leading to
\Psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
given
by
5
5

5

The point transformation
\psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\ni({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)\mapsto{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is
a
submersion
; it is surjective with
\text{rnk}{\text{{{{\small{d}}}}}}\psiup\equiv\text{rnk}\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u)}=3
. It is then lifted to a “canonical submersion”,
\Psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\ni({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\mapsto({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
, which is surjective with
\text{rnk}{\text{{{{\small{d}}}}}}\Psiup\equiv{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}\tfrac{\uppartial({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})}{\uppartial({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})}=6
(for
n\neq 0
). Note the domain and codomains of these maps are not all of some
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
; they are subsets excluding the case
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0
, which, in the projective coordinates, means excluding the case
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0
.

\displaystyle\Psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{6}\;,\hskip 9.24994pt({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})\mapsto({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\hskip 9.24994pt\left\{\;\;\begin{array}[]{lllll}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,u^{n}q^{m}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[5.0pt]
{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u^{n}q^{m}}\big(({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\otimes{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,+\,\tfrac{u}{nq}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\big)\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\right|\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{llll}{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}{\text{{{{\small{d}}}}}}\psiup=3\\[5.0pt]
{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}{\text{{{{\small{d}}}}}}\Psiup=6\end{array}

where
q:=|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|
(not
|\!|{\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}|\!|
) and

{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}:={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}/q
.
Note that we
specify
the above point transformation which then
induces
the above momenta transformation.
Next, we need the Hamiltonian
{{H}}
for the new coordinates,
({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
).
Since the transformation
\Psiup
is not time-dependent,
{{H}}
is given simply by the composition
{{H}}={{K}}\circ\Psiup
. Substituting
\Psiup
from eq.(
1.1.2
) into
{{K}}
from eq.(
1.1.2
) leads to the following
{{H}}
:

\displaystyle{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}q^{2m+2}}\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\Big)\,+\,V({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)

\displaystyle=\;\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}q^{2m+2}}\tfrac{1}{2}({\ell}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2})\,+\,V({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)

(27)

where
p:=|\!|{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|
(not
|\!|{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}|\!|
).

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

It is worth noting that the angular momentum (per unit reduced mass),
{\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
, when expressed in terms of
({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})=({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},u,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
, does not depend on
u
or
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}
, nor the choice of
n
or
m
, and takes the same form in terms of
({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})
or
({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})
:

\displaystyle{\ell}_{ij}:=r_{i}{\nu}_{j}-{\nu}_{i}r_{j}\;=\;q_{i}p_{j}-p_{i}q_{j}

,

\displaystyle{\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}:=\,{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\;=\;{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

,

\displaystyle{\ell}^{2}\,=\,r^{2}{\nu}^{2}-({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}\;=\;q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}

(28)

Inverse Transformation.

eq.(
1.1.2
) is a (local) submersion,
\Psiup:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
, with no unique inverse. However, we show that the new Hamiltonian
{{H}}
for the redundant
8
phase space coordinates permits two additional ‘‘integrals of motion’’
— an abuse of terminology, perhaps
6
6

6

“Integral of motion” is perhaps an abuse of terminology in this context; the functions in eq.(
1.1.2
) are not not true integrals of motion in the sense that they do not represent a conserved quantity for a particle moving in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
(which is the actual system we are modeling). They are, rather, “kinematic constants” which arise from artificially increasing the dimension of phase space.
—
which allow for the inverse transformation. These are:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\footnotesize{integrals}}}\\
{\text{\footnotesize{of motion}}}\end{array}\;\left\{\;\begin{array}[]{ll}k({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}):=|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|\\[5.0pt]
\lambda({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}):={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}-\tfrac{m+1}{n}up_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}k=\{k,{{H}}\}+\tfrac{\uppartial k}{\uppartial{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\bar{{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=0\\[5.0pt]
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\lambda=\{\lambda,{{H}}\}+\tfrac{\uppartial\lambda}{\uppartial{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\bar{{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=0\end{array}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{choose}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}k=k_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1\\[5.0pt]
\lambda=\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0\end{array}

where
{\bar{{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=({\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},\alpha_{u})
are the new generalized nonconservative forces (given in eq.(
57
)).
That is, the above functions
k
and
\lambda
are
constant along a phase space trajectory.
When specifying the initial conditions, we are free to choose/set their initial values to be any
0<k_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{+}}

and
\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
that we like and they will then remain constant at these values for all time.
7
7

7

Any
|\!|{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|=k<0
is incompatible with a Euclidean space and also with
eq.(
1.1.2
).

We will therefore identify these
functions
with their constant
values
.
These constants can be used to obtain the below inverse transformation of eq.(
1.1.2
):

\displaystyle\Psiup^{\scriptscriptstyle{-\!1}}:({\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})\mapsto({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})\quad\left\{\;\;\begin{array}[]{llll}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,k{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
u\,=\,\big(\tfrac{r}{k^{m+1}}\big)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}}\\[5.0pt]
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,\tfrac{r}{k}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}+m{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\otimes{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}+k\lambda{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,n\big(\tfrac{k^{m+1}}{r}\big)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\xRightarrow[\lambda=0]{k=1}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
u\,=\,r^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}}\\[5.0pt]
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,r{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}+m({\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}){\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[5.0pt]
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,\tfrac{n}{r^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}}}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,nr^{\nicefrac{{(n-1)}}{{n}}}\,\dot{r}\end{array}

The above is
not
a true inverse of eq.(
1.1.2
). It is only unique up to some chosen values of
k
and
\lambda
(constants along a phase space trajectory).
The relations on the right follow from our default choices
k=1
and
\lambda=0
.

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

We always choose
k=k_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
and
\lambda=\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
. The former ensures that
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
, and
\lambda=0
is chosen so that
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
will have a “nice” interpretation. Assume these values are chosen for everything that follows in section
1.1
.

The Values of
n
and
m
.

The values of
n\neq 0
and
m
in the point transformation
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=u^{n}q^{m}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
of eq.(
22
), are “knobs” that we may turn to construct different coordinate transformations;

different values of
m
give different
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
while different values of
n
give different
u
and
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}
.
8
8

8

For instance, for
n=1
or
n=-1
:

\displaystyle\text{if \text{$n=1$}}\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}u=r\\
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=\dot{r}\end{array}\right.\qquad\qquad,\qquad\qquad\text{if \text{$n=-1$}}\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}u=1/r\\
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}=-r^{2}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=-r^{2}\dot{r}\end{array}\right.

Note that the momenta
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
conjugate to
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
are not unique.
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
does not depend on the value of
m
but
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
does.
It would seem intuitive to choose
m=0

such that the point transformation in eq.(
22
) is simply the classic
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=u^{n}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
. Indeed, using
m=0
, along with
n=-1
, such that
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=\tfrac{1}{u}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
is precisely the point transformation used by Ferrandiz to develop a canonical version of Burdet’s projective decomposition for the two-body problem.
However, we will usually choose
m=-1
such that the point transformation is
{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=u^{n}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}/q=u^{n}{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
. For these two cases,
m=0
vs.
m=-1
, we note the following differences for the momenta
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
, the integral of motion
\lambda({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})=0
, and any function
f(r)
of the radial distance
r:=|\!|{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}|\!|
(such as a central force potential):

\displaystyle\text{if \text{$m=0$}}\;\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=u^{n}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=r{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[5.0pt]
r=u^{n}q&,\hskip 9.24994ptp^{2}={\ell}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{r}^{2}\\[5.0pt]
{\rotatebox[origin]{10.0}{$\partial$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}f(r)\neq 0&,\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}-\tfrac{1}{n}up_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}=0\end{array}\right.

,

\displaystyle\text{if \text{$m=-1$}}\;\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=u^{n}{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=r^{2}\dot{{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
r=u^{n}&,\hskip 9.24994ptp^{2}={\ell}^{2}\\[5.0pt]
{\rotatebox[origin]{10.0}{$\partial$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}f(r)=0&,\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0\end{array}\right.

where
\dot{{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}}=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\neq{\hat{{\vphantom{v}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{v}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
.
With
m=-1
, note that
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
and
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}={\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\hat{{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}={\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
alone define not only the specific angular momentum but also the local vertical, local horizontal (LVLH) basis associated with the particle’s instantaneous position:

\displaystyle{{\text{\footnotesize{if }}}\;m=-1:}\qquad\qquad\{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\hat{{\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\}\;=\;\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\footnotesize{inertial cartesian coordinate}}}\\
{\text{\footnotesize{representation of LVLH basis}}}\end{array}

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

We will choose
m=-1
(and
n=-1
) as the above properties for the case
m=-1
are well suited for central force dynamics.
As such, the canonical projective coordinates used in this work differ from those given by the Burdet-Ferrándiz transformation, which corresponds to the case
m=0
(and
n=\pm 1
)
[
21
]
.

1.1.3  Hamiltonian Dynamics in Projective Coordinates

Consider the original Hamiltonian and dynamics seen in eq.(
1.1.2
) and let the potential energy function be of the form
V({\vec{{r}}},t)=V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(r)+V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\vec{{r}}},t)
, where
V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}
accounts for any conservative central forces.
The transformed Hamiltonian and dynamics for
({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})
, for arbitrary values of
n
and
m
, are given by

\displaystyle{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\;=\;\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}q^{2m+2}}\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\Big)\,+\,V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})\,+\,V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)

(57)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\dot{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}&,\hskip 18.49988pt\dot{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[5.0pt]
\dot{u}=\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}}&,\hskip 18.49988pt\dot{p}_{u}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial u}+\alpha_{u}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{llll}{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,u^{n}q^{m}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}+m{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\otimes{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}\\[5.0pt]
\alpha_{u}\,=\,{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\tfrac{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\uppartial u}\,=\,nu^{\scalebox{0.7}{$n-1$}}q^{\scalebox{0.7}{$m+1$}}{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}\end{array}

where
{\bar{{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}=({\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},\alpha_{u})
are generalized non-conservative forces (per unit reduced mass) for the new coordinates. The partials of
V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}
(perturbing conservative forces) transform the same as the above and it is useful to combine all perturbing terms as:

\displaystyle{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}:=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,u^{n}q^{m}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}+m{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\otimes{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

,

\displaystyle f_{u}:=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial u}+\alpha_{u}\,=\,nu^{\scalebox{0.7}{$n-1$}}q^{\scalebox{0.7}{$m+1$}}{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

,

\displaystyle{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}:=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}

(62)

where
{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{r}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{r}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}
is the inertial cartesian coordinate vector of the total perturbing force (per unit reduced mass).

Now, the full, explicit, expressions for the above EOMs in eq.(
57
) are rather hideous until we choose values of
n
and
m
.
9
9

9

They are given in full for arbitrary
n
and
m
in section
2.3
.

Leaving
n
arbitrary for now, we again compare the case
m=0
(the BF transformation) and the case
m=-1
(our preference).
For the cases
m=0
, eq.(
57
) leads to

\displaystyle m=0\;\;\left\{\;\;\begin{array}[]{llllll}{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}q^{2}}\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\Big)\,+\,V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}})\,+\,V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\\[10.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{lll}\dot{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}\big({\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}){\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\big)&,\\[10.0pt]
\dot{u}=\tfrac{1}{n^{2}u^{2n-2}}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&,\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}){\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[10.0pt]
\dot{p}_{u}=\tfrac{n}{u^{2n+1}}\big(p^{2}-\tfrac{1}{n^{3}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial u}+f_{u}\end{array}&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{ll}{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,u^{n}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[10.0pt]
f_{u}\,=\,nu^{n-1}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\end{array}\end{array}\right.

where,
after
differentiating
{{H}}
, the above equations of motion have been simplified using
q=k=1
and
\lambda={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}-\tfrac{u}{n}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}=0
.
The Hamiltonian and dynamics for the case
m=-1
may be written as:

\displaystyle m=-1\;\;\left\{\;\;\begin{array}[]{lllllll}{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\Big)+V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(u)+V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\\[10.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{lll}\dot{q}_{i}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}{\ell}_{ij}q_{j}&,\\[10.0pt]
\dot{u}=\tfrac{1}{n^{2}u^{2n-2}}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&,\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{p}_{i}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}{\ell}_{ij}p_{j}\,+\,f_{i}\\[10.0pt]
\dot{p}_{u}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2n+1}}\big({\ell}^{2}+\tfrac{n-1}{n^{3}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial u}+f_{u}\end{array}&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{ll}{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,\tfrac{u^{n}}{q}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\otimes{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[10.0pt]
f_{u}\,=\,nu^{n-1}{\hat{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\end{array}\end{array}\right.

where the above equations have only been simplified using
q=1
so far. Note that, unlike the
m=0
case, for the case
m=-1
then
r=u^{n}
such that
\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=0
; the central forces now appear only on the equation of motion for
p_{u}
(not true for the
m=0
case).
Also, with
m=-1
we conveniently have that the integral
\lambda
is now
\lambda={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0
, which can actually be used to simplify the Hamiltonian itself before differentiating such that the above simplifies further to:

\displaystyle m=-1\;\;\left\{\;\;\begin{array}[]{lllllll}{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}\tfrac{1}{2}\big(q^{2}p^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)+V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(u)+V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\\[10.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{lll}\dot{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}&,\\[10.0pt]
\dot{u}=\tfrac{1}{n^{2}u^{2n-2}}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&,\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}p^{2}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,+\,{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[10.0pt]
\dot{p}_{u}=\tfrac{n}{u^{2n+1}}\big(p^{2}+\tfrac{n-1}{n^{3}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial u}+f_{u}\end{array}&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\\[10.0pt]
\end{array}\end{array}\right.

where, for the case
m=-1
, we have
r=u^{n}
such that
\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}=0
and, conveniently, the integral
\lambda
is now
\lambda={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0
which can actually be used to simplify the Hamiltonian itself before differentiating (as we have done above).

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

We choose
m=-1
for the majority of this work and for the remainder of this summary.

1.1.4  Regularization of Central Force Dynamics

We now use the
extended phase space
to transform the evolution parameter from the time,
t
, to two new evolution parameters,
s
and
\tau
, which are related to the time through the following differential relations:
10
10

10

The evolution parameter
s
is the same as used by Burdet
[
3
,
4
]
.
\tau
is equivalent to the one used by Vitins and Ferrándiz
[
19
,
21
]
.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,r^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s\,=\,u^{2n}{\mathrm{d}}s

,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,\tfrac{r^{2}}{{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\,=\,\tfrac{u^{2n}}{{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}}\tau

,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}s\,=\,{\ell}{\mathrm{d}}\tau

(84)

where
{\ell}^{2}=q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}
.
For orbital motion, the parameter
\tau
is equivalent to the true anomaly up to an additive constant.
The extended Hamiltonian for
s
as the evolution parameter is then

\displaystyle\widetilde{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t,p_{t})\,:=\,\tfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}s}({{H}}+p_{t})\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\big(q^{2}p^{2}-({\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)\,+\,\widetilde{V}^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(u)\,+\,\widetilde{V}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\,+\,u^{2n}p_{t}

(85)

where
\widetilde{V}^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}s}V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=u^{2n}V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}
and likewise for
\widetilde{V}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}
, and where
p_{t}
is the momenta conjugate to the time,
t
. It can be shown that
p_{t}
always has a value of
p_{t}=-{{H}}
such that
\widetilde{{H}}=0
(but
\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial q^{i}}\neq 0\neq\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial p_{i}}
)
[
13
]
. Since the integral
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=\lambda=0
appears in the Hamiltonian as
\lambda^{2}
, it may be dropped from the above
\widetilde{{H}}

such that the effective Hamiltonian and equations of motion for the extended phase space are given
by
11
11

11

Note the equations of motion in eq.(
1.1.4
) are equivalent to
{q^{i}}^{\prime}=u^{2n}\dot{q}^{i}
and
p_{i}^{\prime}=u^{2n}\dot{p}_{i}
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{{H}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},{\bar{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t,p_{t})\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\big(q^{2}p^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)\,+\,\widetilde{V}^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}(u)\,+\,\widetilde{V}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}({\bar{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}},t)\,+\,u^{2n}p_{t}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\acute{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}&\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt\acute{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}+\acute{t}{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,=\,-p^{2}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\,+\,u^{2n}{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[6.0pt]
\acute{u}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}}\,=\,\tfrac{u^{2}}{n^{2}}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt\acute{p}_{u}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial u}+\acute{t}\alpha_{u}\,=\,\tfrac{n}{u}\big(p^{2}+\tfrac{n-1}{n^{3}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)-u^{2n}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial u}\,+\,u^{2n}f_{u}\\[6.0pt]
\acute{t}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial p_{t}}\,=\,u^{2n}&\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt\acute{p}_{t}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{{H}}}{\uppartial t}+\acute{t}\alpha_{t}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{V}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial t}\,-\,{\vphantom{\alphaup}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{\alphaup}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\acute{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}\,-\,\alpha_{u}\acute{u}\end{array}

where
\acute{\square}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}s}=u^{2n}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}t}
, where
({\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}},f_{u})
are the perturbing terms defined in eq.(
62
),
and where
p_{t}=-{{H}}

has been used to eliminate
p_{t}
from the above
\acute{p}_{u}

equation.
12
12

12

Differentiating
\widetilde{{H}}
directly leads to
\acute{p}_{u}=-\tfrac{u}{n^{2}}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}-\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{V}^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial u}-\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{V}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial u}-2nu^{2n-1}p_{t}+u^{2n}\alpha_{u}
, where
\tfrac{\uppartial\widetilde{V}_{i}}{\uppartial u}=2nu^{2n-1}V_{i}+u^{2n}\tfrac{\uppartial V_{i}}{\uppartial u}
. Such that

\displaystyle\acute{p}_{u}\,=\,-\tfrac{u}{n^{2}}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}+u^{2n}(-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}{\uppartial u}-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}}{\uppartial u}+\alpha_{u})-2nu^{2n-1}(V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}+V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}+p_{t})

Substituting
p_{t}=-{{H}}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2n}}\tfrac{1}{2}(q^{2}p^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2})-V^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}-V^{\scalebox{0.7}{$1$}}
into the above leads to the equation for
\acute{p}_{u}
seen in eq.(
1.1.4
).

The canonical equations with
\tau
as the evolution parameter may be obtained simply by dividing eq.(
1.1.4
) by
p={\ell}
.

Angular Motion.

When either
s
or
\tau
is used as the evolution parameter, the canonical equations of motion motion for
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
and
{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
in eq.(
1.1.4
)
are equivalent to second-order equations for a perturbed oscillator:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mathrm{d}{s}^{2}}\,+\,{\ell}^{2}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\;=\;u^{2n}{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}&\;=\;u^{3n}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\otimes{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\\[8.0pt]
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mathrm{d}{\tau}^{2}}\,+\,{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\;=\;\tfrac{u^{2n}}{{\ell}^{2}}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\otimes{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}&\;=\;\tfrac{u^{3n}}{{\ell}^{2}}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}\end{array}

where
{\hat{{\vphantom{{\ell}}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{{\ell}}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}={\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\times{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}
and
{\ell}=p=|\!|\acute{{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}|\!|
, from which we note that the governing equation for
{\ell}
is given by

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}s}\,=\,\tfrac{\mathrm{d}p}{\mathrm{d}s}\,=\,{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}{\mathrm{d}s}\,=\,u^{2n}{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{f}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{f}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}\;=\;u^{3n}{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

,

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\,=\,\tfrac{u^{3n}}{{\ell}}{\hat{{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}

(91)

where we have used
{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\vphantom{p}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{p}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=0
.
If only central forces are present (i.e., if
{\textbf{{{a}}}}=a_{r}{\mkern 1.5mu\hat{\mkern-1.5mu{\textbf{{{r}}}}}}
such that
{\vphantom{a}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{a}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}=a_{r}{\vphantom{q}{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{q}}\mkern-1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu}}}
), then the right-hand-side of eq.(
1.1.4
) and eq.(
91
) vanish;
{\ell}
is an integral of motion such that
{\ell}=p
